<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_relations">Foreign relations</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Iceland" title="Foreign relations of Iceland">Foreign relations of Iceland</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Accession_of_Iceland_to_the_European_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="Accession of Iceland to the European Union">Accession of Iceland to the European Union</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Barack_Obama_and_the_Nordic_leaders_at_White_House_01.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Barack_Obama_and_the_Nordic_leaders_at_White_House_01.jpg/220px-Barack_Obama_and_the_Nordic_leaders_at_White_House_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Barack_Obama_and_the_Nordic_leaders_at_White_House_01.jpg/330px-Barack_Obama_and_the_Nordic_leaders_at_White_House_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Barack_Obama_and_the_Nordic_leaders_at_White_House_01.jpg/440px-Barack_Obama_and_the_Nordic_leaders_at_White_House_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2800" data-file-height="1866" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Barack_Obama_and_the_Nordic_leaders_at_White_House_01.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nordic prime ministers and the president of Finland visiting the White House in 2016, with Iceland's <a href="/wiki/Sigur%C3%B0ur_Ingi_J%C3%B3hannsson" title="Sigurður Ingi Jóhannsson">Sigurður</a> second from the left</div></div></div>
<p>Iceland, which is a member of the <a href="/wiki/UN" class="mw-redirect" title="UN">UN</a>, <a href="/wiki/NATO" title="NATO">NATO</a>, <a href="/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association" title="European Free Trade Association">EFTA</a>, <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Europe" title="Council of Europe">Council of Europe</a>, and <a href="/wiki/OECD" title="OECD">OECD</a>, maintains diplomatic and commercial relations with practically all nations, but its ties with the Nordic countries, Germany, the United States, Canada, and the other NATO nations are particularly close. Historically, due to cultural, economic, and linguistic similarities, Iceland is a <a href="/wiki/Nordic_country" class="mw-redirect" title="Nordic country">Nordic country</a>, and it participates in intergovernmental cooperation through the <a href="/wiki/Nordic_Council" title="Nordic Council">Nordic Council</a>.
</p><p>Iceland is a member of the <a href="/wiki/European_Economic_Area" title="European Economic Area">European Economic Area</a> (EEA), which allows the country access to the single market of the European Union (EU). It was not a member of the EU, but in July 2009, the Icelandic parliament, the Althing, voted in favour of the application for EU membership<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> and officially applied on 17 July 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> However, in 2013, opinion polls showed that many Icelanders were now against joining the EU; following the <a href="/wiki/2013_Icelandic_parliamentary_election" title="2013 Icelandic parliamentary election">2013 Icelandic parliamentary election</a> the two parties that formed the island's new government—the centrist Progressive Party and the right-wing Independence Party—announced they would hold a referendum on EU membership.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> In 2015, Minister for Foreign Affairs <a href="/wiki/Gunnar_Bragi_Sveinsson" title="Gunnar Bragi Sveinsson">Gunnar Bragi Sveinsson</a> informed the EU that Iceland would no longer pursue membership, but the application was not formally withdrawn and there have been subsequent calls for a referendum on the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bilateral_meeting_with_Icelandic_Prime_Minister_(32798372587).jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Bilateral_meeting_with_Icelandic_Prime_Minister_%2832798372587%29.jpg/220px-Bilateral_meeting_with_Icelandic_Prime_Minister_%2832798372587%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="138" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Bilateral_meeting_with_Icelandic_Prime_Minister_%2832798372587%29.jpg/330px-Bilateral_meeting_with_Icelandic_Prime_Minister_%2832798372587%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Bilateral_meeting_with_Icelandic_Prime_Minister_%2832798372587%29.jpg/440px-Bilateral_meeting_with_Icelandic_Prime_Minister_%2832798372587%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5947" data-file-height="3718" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bilateral_meeting_with_Icelandic_Prime_Minister_(32798372587).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Prime Minister of Iceland meets with the <a href="/wiki/First_Minister_of_Scotland" title="First Minister of Scotland">First Minister of Scotland</a> <a href="/wiki/Nicola_Sturgeon" title="Nicola Sturgeon">Nicola Sturgeon</a> at <a href="/wiki/Bute_House" title="Bute House">Bute House</a> in 2019.</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military">Military</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Military_of_Iceland" class="mw-redirect" title="Military of Iceland">Military of Iceland</a></div>
<p>Iceland has no <a href="/wiki/Standing_army" title="Standing army">standing army</a> but has the <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_Coast_Guard" title="Icelandic Coast Guard">Icelandic Coast Guard</a> which also maintains the <a href="/wiki/Iceland_Air_Defence_System" title="Iceland Air Defence System">Iceland Air Defence System</a>, and an <a href="/wiki/Iceland_Crisis_Response_Unit" title="Iceland Crisis Response Unit">Iceland Crisis Response Unit</a> to support peacekeeping missions and perform paramilitary functions.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Iceland_Defense_Force" title="Iceland Defense Force">Iceland Defense Force</a> (IDF) was a <a href="/wiki/Military" title="Military">military</a> <a href="/wiki/Command_(military_formation)" title="Command (military formation)">command</a> of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Armed_Forces" title="United States Armed Forces">United States Armed Forces</a> from 1951 to 2006. The IDF, created at the request of <a href="/wiki/NATO" title="NATO">NATO</a>, came into existence when the United States signed an agreement to provide for the defence of Iceland. The IDF also consisted of civilian Icelanders and military members of other NATO nations. The IDF was downsized after the end of the <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> and the <a href="/wiki/U.S._Air_Force" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Air Force">U.S. Air Force</a> maintained four to six <a href="/wiki/Interceptor_aircraft" title="Interceptor aircraft">interceptor aircraft</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Naval_Air_Station_Keflavik" title="Naval Air Station Keflavik">Naval Air Station Keflavik</a> until they were withdrawn on 30 September 2006. Since May 2008, NATO nations have periodically deployed fighters to patrol Icelandic airspace under the <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_Air_Policing" title="Icelandic Air Policing">Icelandic Air Policing</a> mission.<sup id="cite_ref-French_Air_Force_in_Iceland_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-French_Air_Force_in_Iceland-127">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-NATO_ACO_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NATO_ACO-128">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Iceland supported the <a href="/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq" title="2003 invasion of Iraq">2003 invasion of Iraq</a> despite much domestic controversy, deploying a Coast Guard <a href="/wiki/Explosive_Ordnance_Disposal" class="mw-redirect" title="Explosive Ordnance Disposal">EOD</a> team to Iraq,<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> which was replaced later by members of the <a href="/wiki/Iceland_Crisis_Response_Unit" title="Iceland Crisis Response Unit">Iceland Crisis Response Unit</a>. Iceland has also participated in the <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)" title="War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)">conflict in Afghanistan</a><sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/1999_NATO_bombing_of_Yugoslavia" class="mw-redirect" title="1999 NATO bombing of Yugoslavia">1999 NATO bombing of Yugoslavia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Despite the ongoing financial crisis the first <a href="/wiki/ICGV_%C3%9E%C3%B3r_(2009)" title="ICGV Þór (2009)">new patrol ship</a> in decades was launched on 29 April 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Iceland was the neutral host of the historic 1986 <a href="/wiki/Reykjav%C3%ADk_Summit" title="Reykjavík Summit">Reagan–Gorbachev summit</a> in Reykjavík, which set the stage for the end of the <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a>. Iceland's principal historical international disputes involved disagreements over <a href="/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone" title="Exclusive economic zone">exclusive economic zones</a>. Conflict with the United Kingdom led to a series of so-called <a href="/wiki/Cod_Wars" title="Cod Wars">Cod Wars</a>, which included confrontations between the Icelandic Coast Guard and the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">Royal Navy</a> over British fishermen: in 1952–1956 due to the extension of Iceland's fishing zone from 3 to 4&#160;<a href="/wiki/Nautical_mile" title="Nautical mile">nmi</a> (5.6 to 7.4&#160;km; 3.5 to 4.6&#160;mi), in 1958–1961 following a further extension to 12&#160;nmi (22.2&#160;km; 13.8&#160;mi), in 1972–1973 with another extension to 50&#160;nmi (92.6&#160;km; 57.5&#160;mi), and in 1975–1976 after another extension to 200&#160;nmi (370.4&#160;km; 230.2&#160;mi).<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the 2011 <a href="/wiki/Global_Peace_Index" title="Global Peace Index">Global Peace Index</a>, Iceland is the most peaceful country in the world, due to its lack of armed forces, low crime rate and high level of socio-political stability.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Iceland is listed in <i><a href="/wiki/Guinness_World_Records" title="Guinness World Records">Guinness World Records</a></i> as the "country ranked most at peace" and the "lowest military spending per capita".<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Environmental_policy">Environmental policy</span></h3>
<p>Iceland's official governmental goal is to cut <a href="/wiki/Greenhouse_gas" title="Greenhouse gas">greenhouse gas</a> emissions by 40% by the year 2030 and reach <a href="/wiki/Carbon_neutrality" title="Carbon neutrality">carbon neutrality</a> by the year 2040.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Icelandic Forest Service and other forestry groups promote large-scale reforestation in the country. Before the first human settlement, 25–40% of the country was covered by forest. The Vikings cut them for fuel, building, and making iron. Due to the reforestation efforts, the forest cover of Iceland increased six-fold since the 1990s. This helps to offset carbon emissions, prevent sand storms and increase the productivity of farms.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Iceland" title="Economy of Iceland">Economy of Iceland</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:292px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Iceland_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Iceland_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/290px-Iceland_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="290" height="189" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Iceland_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/435px-Iceland_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Iceland_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/580px-Iceland_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="715" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Iceland_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A proportional representation of Iceland's exports, 2019</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Akureyri.JPG" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/Akureyri.JPG/220px-Akureyri.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/Akureyri.JPG/330px-Akureyri.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/Akureyri.JPG/440px-Akureyri.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Akureyri.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Akureyri" title="Akureyri">Akureyri</a> is the largest town in Iceland outside the <a href="/wiki/Capital_Region_(Iceland)" title="Capital Region (Iceland)">Capital Region</a>. Most rural towns are based on the fishing industry, which provides 40% of Iceland's exports.</div></div></div>
<p>In 2007, Iceland was the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(nominal)" title="List of countries by GDP (nominal)">seventh-most productive country in the world per capita</a> (US$54,858), and the fifth-most productive by <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)_per_capita" title="List of countries by GDP (PPP) per capita">GDP</a> at <a href="/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity" title="Purchasing power parity">purchasing power parity</a> ($40,112). About 85 percent of the total <a href="/wiki/Primary_energy" title="Primary energy">primary energy</a> supply in Iceland is derived from domestically produced renewable energy sources.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Use of abundant <a href="/wiki/Hydroelectricity" title="Hydroelectricity">hydroelectric</a> and <a href="/wiki/Geothermal_power" title="Geothermal power">geothermal power</a> has made Iceland the world's largest electricity producer per capita.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> As a result of its commitment to renewable energy, the 2016 Global Green Economy Index ranked Iceland among the top 10 greenest economies in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-ggei_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ggei-140">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> Historically, Iceland's economy depended heavily on fishing, which still provides 40% of export earnings and employs 7% of the workforce.<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_Govt_73-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA_Govt-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The economy is vulnerable to declining fish stocks and falls in world prices for its main material exports: fish and fish products, aluminium, and <a href="/wiki/Ferrosilicon" title="Ferrosilicon">ferrosilicon</a>. <a href="/wiki/Whaling_in_Iceland" title="Whaling in Iceland">Whaling in Iceland</a> has been historically significant. Iceland still relies heavily on fishing, but its importance is diminishing from an export share of 90% in the 1960s to 40% in 2006.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Until the 20th century, Iceland was a fairly poor country. It is now one of the most developed countries in the world. Strong economic growth led Iceland to be ranked <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index" title="List of countries by Human Development Index">third</a> in the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>' <a href="/wiki/Human_Development_Index" title="Human Development Index">Human Development Index</a> report for 2021/2022.<sup id="cite_ref-HDI_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HDI-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>  According to the Economist Intelligence Index of 2011, Iceland had the second-highest quality of life in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Based on the <a href="/wiki/Gini_coefficient" title="Gini coefficient">Gini coefficient</a>, Iceland also has one of the lowest rates of income inequality in the world,<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-143">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> and when <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_inequality-adjusted_HDI" class="mw-redirect" title="List of countries by inequality-adjusted HDI">adjusted for inequality</a>, its HDI ranking is sixth.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> Iceland's unemployment rate has declined consistently since the crisis, with 4.8% of the labour force being unemployed as of June&#160;2012<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iceland&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, compared to 6% in 2011 and 8.1% in 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_Govt_73-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA_Govt-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Many political parties remain opposed to EU membership, primarily due to Icelanders' concern about losing control over their natural resources (particularly fisheries).<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> The national currency of Iceland is the <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_kr%C3%B3na" title="Icelandic króna">Icelandic króna</a> (ISK). Iceland is the only country in the world to have a population under two million yet still have a floating exchange rate and an independent monetary policy.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A poll released on 5 March 2010 by Capacent <a href="/wiki/Gallup_(company)" class="mw-redirect" title="Gallup (company)">Gallup</a> showed that 31% of respondents were in favour of adopting the <a href="/wiki/Euro" title="Euro">euro</a> and 69% opposed.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Another Capacent Gallup poll conducted in February 2012 found that 67.4% of Icelanders would reject EU membership in a referendum.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Iceland's economy has been diversifying into manufacturing and service industries in the last decade, including software production, <a href="/wiki/Biotechnology" title="Biotechnology">biotechnology</a>, and finance; industry accounts for around a quarter of economic activity, while services comprise close to 70%.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> The tourism sector is expanding, especially in <a href="/wiki/Ecotourism" title="Ecotourism">ecotourism</a> and whale-watching. On average, Iceland receives around 1.1&#160;million visitors annually, which is more than three times the native population.<sup id="cite_ref-Iceland_–_OECD_Better_Life_Index_112-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Iceland_–_OECD_Better_Life_Index-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> 1.7 million people visited Iceland in 2016, 3 times more than the number that came in 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Iceland's agriculture industry, accounting for 5.4% of GDP,<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_Govt_73-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA_Govt-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> consists mainly of <a href="/wiki/Potato" title="Potato">potatoes</a>, green vegetables (in <a href="/wiki/Greenhouse" title="Greenhouse">greenhouses</a>), <a href="/wiki/Mutton" class="mw-redirect" title="Mutton">mutton</a>, and dairy products.<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_Govt_73-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA_Govt-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The financial centre is <a href="/wiki/Borgart%C3%BAn" title="Borgartún">Borgartún</a> in Reykjavík, which hosts a large number of companies and three investment banks. Iceland's <a href="/wiki/Stock_market" title="Stock market">stock market</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Iceland_Stock_Exchange" class="mw-redirect" title="Iceland Stock Exchange">Iceland Stock Exchange</a> (ISE), was established in 1985.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Iceland is ranked 27th in the 2012 <a href="/wiki/Index_of_Economic_Freedom" title="Index of Economic Freedom">Index of Economic Freedom</a>, lower than in prior years but still among the freest in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> As of 2016<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iceland&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, it ranks 29th in the <a href="/wiki/World_Economic_Forum" title="World Economic Forum">World Economic Forum</a>'s Global Competitive Index, one place lower than in 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> According to the <a href="/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index" title="Global Innovation Index">Global Innovation Index</a>, Iceland is the 20th most innovative country in the world in 2022.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Unlike most Western European countries, Iceland has a <a href="/wiki/Flat_tax" title="Flat tax">flat tax</a> system: the main personal income tax rate is a flat 22.75% and combined with municipal taxes, the total tax rate equals no more than 35.7%, not including the many available deductions.<sup id="cite_ref-economicfreedom2008_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-economicfreedom2008-157">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Corporate_tax" title="Corporate tax">corporate tax</a> rate is a flat 18%, one of the lowest in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-economicfreedom2008_157-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-economicfreedom2008-157">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> There is also a <a href="/wiki/Value_added_tax" class="mw-redirect" title="Value added tax">value added tax</a>, whereas a net <a href="/wiki/Wealth_tax" title="Wealth tax">wealth tax</a> was eliminated in 2006. Employment regulations are relatively flexible and the labour market is one of the freest in the world. Property rights are strong and Iceland is one of the few countries where they are applied to <a href="/wiki/Fisheries_management" title="Fisheries management">fishery management</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-economicfreedom2008_157-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-economicfreedom2008-157">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Like other <a href="/wiki/Welfare_state" title="Welfare state">welfare states</a>, taxpayers pay various subsidies to each other, but with spending being less than in most European countries.
</p><p>Despite low tax rates, agricultural assistance is the highest among OECD countries and a potential impediment to structural change. Also, health care and education spending have relatively poor returns by OECD measures, though improvements have been made in both areas. The OECD <i>Economic Survey of Iceland 2008</i> highlighted Iceland's challenges in currency and macroeconomic policy.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> There was a <a href="/wiki/Currency_crisis" title="Currency crisis">currency crisis</a> that started in the spring of 2008, and on 6 October trading in Iceland's banks was suspended as the government battled to save the economy.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> An assessment by the OECD 2011<sup id="cite_ref-oecd2011_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oecd2011-160">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> determined that Iceland has made progress in many areas, particularly in creating a sustainable fiscal policy and restoring the health of the financial sector; however, challenges remain in making the fishing industry more efficient and sustainable, as well as in improving monetary policy to address inflation.<sup id="cite_ref-oecd2011_160-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oecd2011-160">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Iceland's public debt has decreased since the economic crisis, and as of 2015<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iceland&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> is the 31st-highest in the world by proportion of national GDP.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_contraction">Economic contraction</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/2008%E2%80%932011_Icelandic_financial_crisis" title="2008–2011 Icelandic financial crisis">2008–2011 Icelandic financial crisis</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:W16_Protesters_2659.JPG" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/W16_Protesters_2659.JPG/220px-W16_Protesters_2659.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/W16_Protesters_2659.JPG/330px-W16_Protesters_2659.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/W16_Protesters_2659.JPG/440px-W16_Protesters_2659.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2075" data-file-height="1390" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:W16_Protesters_2659.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/2009_Icelandic_financial_crisis_protests" title="2009 Icelandic financial crisis protests">2009 Icelandic financial crisis protests</a></div></div></div>
<p>Iceland was hit especially hard by the <a href="/wiki/Great_Recession" title="Great Recession">Great Recession</a> that began in December 2007 because of the failure of its banking system and a subsequent economic crisis. Before the crash of the country's three largest banks, <a href="/wiki/Glitnir_(bank)" title="Glitnir (bank)">Glitnir</a>, <a href="/wiki/Landsbanki" title="Landsbanki">Landsbanki</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kaupthing" class="mw-redirect" title="Kaupthing">Kaupthing</a>, their combined debt exceeded approximately six times the nation's gross domestic product of €14&#160;billion ($19&#160;billion).<sup id="cite_ref-bbc.1_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbc.1-162">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> In October 2008, the Icelandic parliament passed emergency legislation to minimise the impact of the financial crisis. The Financial Supervisory Authority of Iceland used permission granted by the emergency legislation to take over the domestic operations of the three largest banks.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Icelandic officials, including central bank governor <a href="/wiki/Dav%C3%AD%C3%B0_Oddsson" title="Davíð Oddsson">Davíð Oddsson</a>, stated that the state did not intend to take over any of the banks' foreign debts or assets. Instead, new banks were established to take on the domestic operations of the banks, and the old banks were to be run into bankruptcy.
</p><p>On 28 October 2008, the Icelandic government raised interest rates to 18% (as of August&#160;2019<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iceland&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, it was 3.5%), a move forced in part by the terms of acquiring a loan from <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a> (IMF). After the rate hike, trading on the Icelandic króna finally resumed on the open market, with a valuation at around 250 ISK per euro, less than one-third the value of the 1:70 exchange rate during most of 2008, and a significant drop from the 1:150 exchange ratio of the week before. On 20 November 2008, the <a href="/wiki/Nordic_countries" title="Nordic countries">Nordic countries</a> agreed to lend Iceland $2.5&#160;billion.<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 26 January 2009, the coalition government collapsed due to public dissent over the handling of the financial crisis. A new left-wing government was formed a week later and immediately set about removing Central Bank governor Davíð Oddsson and his aides from the bank through changes in the law. Davíð was removed on 26 February 2009 in the wake of protests outside the Central Bank.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thousands of Icelanders left the country after the collapse, many of those moving to Norway. In 2005, 293 people moved from Iceland to Norway; in 2009, the figure was 1,625.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> In April 2010, the Icelandic Parliament's Special Investigation Commission published the findings of its investigation,<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> revealing the extent of control fraud in this crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> By June 2012, Landsbanki managed to repay about half of the <a href="/wiki/Icesave_dispute" title="Icesave dispute">Icesave</a> debt.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Bloomberg_L.P." title="Bloomberg L.P.">Bloomberg</a> in 2014, Iceland was on the trajectory of 2% unemployment as a result of crisis-management decisions made back in 2008, including allowing the banks to fail.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transport">Transport</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transport_in_Iceland" title="Transport in Iceland">Transport in Iceland</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Route1(iceland).png" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Route1%28iceland%29.png/220px-Route1%28iceland%29.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Route1%28iceland%29.png/330px-Route1%28iceland%29.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/ce/Route1%28iceland%29.png 2x" data-file-width="350" data-file-height="253" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Route1(iceland).png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Route_1_(Iceland)" title="Route 1 (Iceland)">The Ring Road of Iceland</a> and some towns it passes through: 1. <a href="/wiki/Reykjav%C3%ADk" title="Reykjavík">Reykjavík</a>, 2. <a href="/wiki/Borgarnes" title="Borgarnes">Borgarnes</a>, 3. <a href="/wiki/Bl%C3%B6ndu%C3%B3s" title="Blönduós">Blönduós</a>, 4. <a href="/wiki/Akureyri" title="Akureyri">Akureyri</a>, 5. <a href="/wiki/Egilssta%C3%B0ir" title="Egilsstaðir">Egilsstaðir</a>, 6. <a href="/wiki/H%C3%B6fn" title="Höfn">Höfn</a>, 7. <a href="/wiki/Selfoss_(town)" title="Selfoss (town)">Selfoss</a></div></div></div>
<p>Iceland has a high level of car ownership per capita, with a car for every 1.5 inhabitants; it is the main form of transport.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Iceland has 13,034&#160;km (8,099&#160;mi) of administered roads, of which 4,617&#160;km (2,869&#160;mi) are paved and 8,338&#160;km (5,181&#160;mi) are not. A great number of roads remain unpaved, mostly little-used rural roads. The road speed limits are 30&#160;km/h (19&#160;mph) and 50&#160;km/h (31&#160;mph) in towns, 80&#160;km/h (50&#160;mph) on gravel country roads and 90&#160;km/h (56&#160;mph) on hard-surfaced roads.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Route_1_(Iceland)" title="Route 1 (Iceland)">Route 1</a>, or the Ring Road (<a href="/wiki/Icelandic_language" title="Icelandic language">Icelandic</a>: <i lang="is">Þjóðvegur 1</i> or <span title="Icelandic-language text"><i lang="is">Hringvegur</i></span>), was completed in 1974, and is the main road that runs around Iceland and connects all the inhabited parts of the island, with the interior of the island being uninhabited. This paved road is 1,332&#160;km (828&#160;mi)<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> long with one lane in each direction, except near larger towns and cities and in the <a href="/wiki/Hvalfj%C3%B6r%C3%B0ur_Tunnel" title="Hvalfjörður Tunnel">Hvalfjörður Tunnel</a> where it has more lanes. Many bridges on it, especially in the north and east, are single lanes and made of timber or steel.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Keflav%C3%ADk_International_Airport" title="Keflavík International Airport">Keflavík International Airport</a> (KEF)<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> is the largest airport and the main aviation hub for international passenger transport. It serves several international and domestic airline companies.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> KEF is in the vicinity of the larger metropolitan capital areas, 49&#160;km (30&#160;mi)<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> to the WSW of Reykjavík centre, and public bus services are available.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Iceland has no passenger railways.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Reykjav%C3%ADk_Airport" title="Reykjavík Airport">Reykjavík Airport</a> (RKV)<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> is the second-largest airport, located just 1.5&#160;km from the capital centre. RKV serves general aviation traffic and has daily or regular domestic flights to 12 local townships within Iceland.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> RKV also serves international flights to <a href="/wiki/Greenland" title="Greenland">Greenland</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Faroe_Islands" title="Faroe Islands">Faroe Islands</a>, business and private aeroplanes along with aviation training.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Akureyri_Airport" title="Akureyri Airport">Akureyri Airport</a> (AEY)<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Egilssta%C3%B0ir_Airport" title="Egilsstaðir Airport">Egilsstaðir Airport</a> (EGS)<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> are two other domestic airports with limited international service capacity. There are a total of 103 registered airports and airfields in Iceland; most of them are unpaved and located in rural areas. The second-longest runway is at Geitamelur, a four-runway glider field around 100&#160;km (62&#160;mi) east of Reykjavík.
</p><p>Several ferry services provide regular access to various outpost communities or shorten travel distances.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Energy">Energy</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Energy_in_Iceland" title="Energy in Iceland">Energy in Iceland</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:NesjavellirPowerPlant_edit2.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/NesjavellirPowerPlant_edit2.jpg/220px-NesjavellirPowerPlant_edit2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/NesjavellirPowerPlant_edit2.jpg/330px-NesjavellirPowerPlant_edit2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/NesjavellirPowerPlant_edit2.jpg/440px-NesjavellirPowerPlant_edit2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="1600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:NesjavellirPowerPlant_edit2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Nesjavellir_Geothermal_Power_Station" title="Nesjavellir Geothermal Power Station">Nesjavellir Geothermal Power Station</a> services the <a href="/wiki/Capital_Region_(Iceland)" title="Capital Region (Iceland)">Capital Region</a>'s hot water and electricity needs. Virtually all of Iceland's electricity comes from renewable resources.<sup id="cite_ref-energy_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-energy-184">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Iceland_electricity_production.svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Iceland_electricity_production.svg/220px-Iceland_electricity_production.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="201" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Iceland_electricity_production.svg/330px-Iceland_electricity_production.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Iceland_electricity_production.svg/440px-Iceland_electricity_production.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="639" data-file-height="585" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Iceland_electricity_production.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Iceland electricity production by source</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Renewable_energy" title="Renewable energy">Renewable sources</a>—<a href="/wiki/Geothermal_power" title="Geothermal power">geothermal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hydropower" title="Hydropower">hydropower</a>—provide effectively all of Iceland's electricity<sup id="cite_ref-energy_184-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-energy-184">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> and around 85% of the nation's total primary energy consumption,<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> with most of the remainder consisting of imported oil products used in transportation and in the fishing fleet.<sup id="cite_ref-statice1_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-statice1-186">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> A 2000 report from the University of Iceland suggested that Iceland could potentially convert from oil to hydrogen power by 2040.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> Iceland's largest geothermal power plants are <a href="/wiki/Hellishei%C3%B0i_Power_Station" title="Hellisheiði Power Station">Hellisheiði</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nesjavellir_Geothermal_Power_Station" title="Nesjavellir Geothermal Power Station">Nesjavellir</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> while <a href="/wiki/K%C3%A1rahnj%C3%BAkar_Hydropower_Plant" title="Kárahnjúkar Hydropower Plant">Kárahnjúkar Hydropower Plant</a> is the country's largest hydroelectric power station.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> When the Kárahnjúkavirkjun started operating, Iceland became the world's largest electricity producer per capita.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> Iceland is one of the few countries that have filling stations dispensing hydrogen fuel for cars powered by <a href="/wiki/Fuel_cell" title="Fuel cell">fuel cells</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite this, Icelanders emitted 16.9&#160;tonnes of CO<sub>2</sub> per capita in 2016, the highest among EFTA and EU members, mainly resulting from transport and aluminium smelting.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, in 2010, Iceland was reported by <i>Guinness World Records</i> as "the Greenest Country", reaching the highest score by the <a href="/wiki/Environmental_Sustainability_Index" class="mw-redirect" title="Environmental Sustainability Index">Environmental Sustainability Index</a>, which measures a country's water use, biodiversity and adoption of clean energies, with a score of 93.5/100.<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 22 January 2009, Iceland announced its first round of <a href="/wiki/Oil_platform" title="Oil platform">offshore</a> licences for companies wanting to conduct <a href="/wiki/Hydrocarbon_exploration" title="Hydrocarbon exploration">hydrocarbon exploration</a> and production in a region northeast of Iceland, known as the <a href="/wiki/Jan_Mayen_Microcontinent#Economic_resources" title="Jan Mayen Microcontinent">Dreki area</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> Three exploration licences were awarded but all were subsequently relinquished.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education_and_science">Education and science</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Iceland" title="Education in Iceland">Education in Iceland</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Iceland" title="Science and technology in Iceland">Science and technology in Iceland</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Menntask%C3%B3linn_%C3%AD_Reykjav%C3%ADk_(main_building,_2004).jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Menntask%C3%B3linn_%C3%AD_Reykjav%C3%ADk_%28main_building%2C_2004%29.jpg/220px-Menntask%C3%B3linn_%C3%AD_Reykjav%C3%ADk_%28main_building%2C_2004%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Menntask%C3%B3linn_%C3%AD_Reykjav%C3%ADk_%28main_building%2C_2004%29.jpg/330px-Menntask%C3%B3linn_%C3%AD_Reykjav%C3%ADk_%28main_building%2C_2004%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Menntask%C3%B3linn_%C3%AD_Reykjav%C3%ADk_%28main_building%2C_2004%29.jpg/440px-Menntask%C3%B3linn_%C3%AD_Reykjav%C3%ADk_%28main_building%2C_2004%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Menntask%C3%B3linn_%C3%AD_Reykjav%C3%ADk_(main_building,_2004).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Reykjavík Junior College (<span title="Icelandic-language text"><i lang="is"><a href="/wiki/Menntask%C3%B3linn_%C3%AD_Reykjav%C3%ADk" title="Menntaskólinn í Reykjavík">Menntaskólinn í Reykjavík</a></i></span>), located in downtown Reykjavík, is the oldest <a href="/wiki/Gymnasium_(school)" title="Gymnasium (school)">gymnasium</a> in Iceland.</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Education,_Science_and_Culture_(Iceland)" title="Ministry of Education, Science and Culture (Iceland)">Ministry of Education, Science and Culture</a> is responsible for the policies and methods that schools must use, and they issue the National Curriculum Guidelines. However, playschools, primary schools, and lower secondary schools are funded and administered by the municipalities. The government does allow citizens to <a href="/wiki/Homeschooling" title="Homeschooling">home educate</a> their children, however, under a very strict set of demands.<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> Students must adhere closely to the government-mandated curriculum, and the parent teaching must acquire a government approved teaching certificate.
</p><p>Nursery school, or <span title="Icelandic-language text"><i lang="is">leikskóli</i></span>, is non-compulsory education for children younger than six years and is the first step in the education system. The current legislation concerning playschools was passed in 1994. They are also responsible for ensuring that the curriculum is suitable to make the transition into compulsory education as easy as possible.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Compulsory education, or <span title="Icelandic-language text"><i lang="is">grunnskóli</i></span>, comprises primary and lower secondary education, which often is conducted at the same institution. Education is mandatory by law for children aged from 6 to 16&#160;years. The school year lasts nine months, beginning between 21 August and 1 September, and ending between 31 May and 10 June. The minimum number of school days was once 170, but after a new teachers' wage contract, it increased to 180. Lessons take place five days a week. All public schools have mandatory education in Christianity, although an exemption may be considered by the <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Education,_Science_and_Culture_(Iceland)" title="Minister of Education, Science and Culture (Iceland)">Minister of Education</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Jonathan_Fox_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jonathan_Fox-202">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upper secondary education, or <span title="Icelandic-language text"><i lang="is">framhaldsskóli</i></span>, follows lower secondary education. These schools are also known as <a href="/wiki/Gymnasium_(school)" title="Gymnasium (school)">gymnasia</a> in English. Though not compulsory, everyone who has had a compulsory education has the right to upper secondary education. This stage of education is governed by the Upper Secondary School Act of 1996. All schools in Iceland are mixed-sex schools. The largest seat of higher education is the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Iceland" title="University of Iceland">University of Iceland</a>, which has its main campus in central Reykjavík. Other schools offering university-level instruction include <a href="/wiki/Reykjav%C3%ADk_University" title="Reykjavík University">Reykjavík University</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Akureyri" title="University of Akureyri">University of Akureyri</a>, <a href="/wiki/Agricultural_University_of_Iceland" title="Agricultural University of Iceland">Agricultural University of Iceland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bifr%C3%B6st_University" title="Bifröst University">Bifröst University</a>.
</p><p>An OECD assessment found that 64% of Icelanders aged 25–64 have earned the equivalent of a high-school degree, which is lower than the OECD average of 73%. Among 25- to 34-year-olds, only 69% have earned the equivalent of a high-school degree, significantly lower than the OECD average of 80%.<sup id="cite_ref-Iceland_–_OECD_Better_Life_Index_112-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Iceland_–_OECD_Better_Life_Index-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, Iceland's education system is considered excellent: the <a href="/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment" title="Programme for International Student Assessment">Programme for International Student Assessment</a> ranks it as the 16th best performing, above the OECD average.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> Students were particularly proficient in reading and mathematics.
</p><p>According to a 2013 <a href="/wiki/Eurostat" title="Eurostat">Eurostat</a> report by the <a href="/wiki/European_Commission" title="European Commission">European Commission</a>, Iceland spends around 3.11% of its GDP on scientific research and development (R&amp;D), over 1 percentage point higher than the EU average of 2.03%, and has set a target of 4% to reach by 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Iceland was ranked 17th in the <a href="/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index" title="Global Innovation Index">Global Innovation Index</a> in 2021, up from 20th in 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> A 2010 <a href="/wiki/UNESCO" title="UNESCO">UNESCO</a> report found that out of 72 countries that spend the most on R&amp;D (US$100&#160;million or more), Iceland ranked ninth by proportion of GDP, tied with Taiwan, Switzerland, and Germany and ahead of France, the United Kingdom and Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For statistics on demographics, see <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Iceland" title="Demographics of Iceland">Demographics of Iceland</a>.</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Icelanders" title="Icelanders">Icelanders</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Reykjav%C3%ADk_s%C3%A9%C3%B0_%C3%BAr_Hallgr%C3%ADmskirkju_6.JPG" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Reykjav%C3%ADk_s%C3%A9%C3%B0_%C3%BAr_Hallgr%C3%ADmskirkju_6.JPG/220px-Reykjav%C3%ADk_s%C3%A9%C3%B0_%C3%BAr_Hallgr%C3%ADmskirkju_6.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Reykjav%C3%ADk_s%C3%A9%C3%B0_%C3%BAr_Hallgr%C3%ADmskirkju_6.JPG/330px-Reykjav%C3%ADk_s%C3%A9%C3%B0_%C3%BAr_Hallgr%C3%ADmskirkju_6.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Reykjav%C3%ADk_s%C3%A9%C3%B0_%C3%BAr_Hallgr%C3%ADmskirkju_6.JPG/440px-Reykjav%C3%ADk_s%C3%A9%C3%B0_%C3%BAr_Hallgr%C3%ADmskirkju_6.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3296" data-file-height="2472" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Reykjav%C3%ADk_s%C3%A9%C3%B0_%C3%BAr_Hallgr%C3%ADmskirkju_6.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Reykjav%C3%ADk" title="Reykjavík">Reykjavík</a>, Iceland's largest metropolitan area and the centre of the <a href="/wiki/Capital_Region_(Iceland)" title="Capital Region (Iceland)">Capital Region</a> which, with a population of 233,034, makes for 64% of Iceland's population. (Numbers from 2020)</div></div></div>
<p>The original population of Iceland was of <a href="/wiki/Nordic_race" title="Nordic race">Nordic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gaels" title="Gaels">Gaelic</a> origin. This is evident from literary evidence dating from the settlement period as well as from later scientific studies such as <a href="/wiki/Blood_type" title="Blood type">blood type</a> and genetic analyses. One such genetic study indicated that the majority of the male settlers were of Nordic origin while the majority of the women were of Gaelic origin, meaning many settlers of Iceland were Norsemen who brought Gaelic slaves with them.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Iceland has extensive genealogical records dating back to the late 17th century and fragmentary records extending back to the <a href="/wiki/Settlement_of_Iceland" title="Settlement of Iceland">Age of Settlement</a>. The biopharmaceutical company <a href="/wiki/DeCODE_genetics" title="DeCODE genetics">deCODE genetics</a> has funded the creation of a <a href="/wiki/Genealogy" title="Genealogy">genealogy</a> database that is intended to cover all of Iceland's known inhabitants. It views the database, called <i><a href="/wiki/%C3%8Dslendingab%C3%B3k_(genealogical_database)" title="Íslendingabók (genealogical database)">Íslendingabók</a></i>, as a valuable tool for conducting research on genetic diseases, given the relative isolation of Iceland's population.
</p><p>The population of the island is believed to have varied from 40,000 to 60,000 in the period ranging from initial settlement until the mid-19th century. During that time, cold winters, ash fall from volcanic eruptions, and <a href="/wiki/Bubonic_plague" title="Bubonic plague">bubonic plagues</a> adversely affected the population several times.<sup id="cite_ref-tomasson_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tomasson-19">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> There were 37 <a href="/wiki/Famine" title="Famine">famine</a> years in Iceland between 1500 and 1804.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> The first census was carried out in 1703 and revealed that the population was then 50,358. After the destructive volcanic eruptions of the <a href="/wiki/Laki" title="Laki">Laki volcano</a> during 1783–1784, the population reached a low of about 40,000.<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> Improving living conditions have triggered a rapid increase in population since the mid-19th century—from about 60,000 in 1850 to 320,000 in 2008. Iceland has a relatively young population for a developed country, with one out of five people being 14&#160;years old or younger. With a fertility rate of 2.1, Iceland is one of only a few European countries with a birth rate <a href="/wiki/Replacement_fertility_rate" class="mw-redirect" title="Replacement fertility rate">sufficient for long-term population growth</a> (see table below).<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2007, 33,678 people (13.5% of the total population) living in Iceland had been born abroad, including children of Icelandic parents living abroad. Around 19,000 people (6% of the population) held foreign citizenship. <a href="/wiki/Polish_diaspora" title="Polish diaspora">Polish</a> people make up the largest minority group by a considerable margin and still form the bulk of the foreign workforce.<sup id="cite_ref-citizenship_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-citizenship-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> About 8,000 Poles now live in Iceland, 1,500 of them in <a href="/wiki/Fjar%C3%B0abygg%C3%B0" title="Fjarðabyggð">Fjarðabyggð</a> where they make up 75% of the workforce who are constructing the Fjarðarál aluminium plant.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Large-scale construction projects in the east of Iceland (see <a href="/wiki/K%C3%A1rahnj%C3%BAkar_Hydropower_Plant" title="Kárahnjúkar Hydropower Plant">Kárahnjúkar Hydropower Plant</a>) have also brought in many people whose stay is expected to be temporary. Many Polish immigrants were also considering leaving in 2008 as a result of the <a href="/wiki/2008%E2%80%932011_Icelandic_financial_crisis" title="2008–2011 Icelandic financial crisis">Icelandic financial crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The southwest corner of Iceland is by far the most densely populated region. It is also the location of the capital Reykjavík, the northernmost national capital in the world. More than 70 percent of Iceland's population lives in the southwest corner (<a href="/wiki/Capital_Region_(Iceland)" title="Capital Region (Iceland)">Greater Reykjavík</a> and the nearby <a href="/wiki/Southern_Peninsula_(Iceland)" title="Southern Peninsula (Iceland)">Southern Peninsula</a>), which covers less than two percent of Iceland's land area. The largest town outside Greater Reykjavík is <a href="/wiki/Reykjanesb%C3%A6r" title="Reykjanesbær">Reykjanesbær</a>, which is located on the Southern Peninsula, less than 50&#160;km (31&#160;mi) from the capital. The largest town outside the southwest corner is <a href="/wiki/Akureyri" title="Akureyri">Akureyri</a> in northern Iceland.
</p><p>Some 500 Icelanders under the leadership of <a href="/wiki/Erik_the_Red" title="Erik the Red">Erik the Red</a> settled <a href="/wiki/Greenland" title="Greenland">Greenland</a> in the late tenth century.<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> The total population reached a high point of perhaps 5,000, and developed independent institutions before disappearing by 1500.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> People from Greenland attempted to set up a settlement at <a href="/wiki/Vinland" title="Vinland">Vinland</a> in North America, but abandoned it in the face of hostility from the indigenous residents.<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Emigration of Icelanders to the United States and Canada began in the 1870s. As of 2006<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iceland&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, Canada had over 88,000 people of <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="Icelandic Canadian">Icelandic</a> descent,<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> while there are more than 40,000 Americans of <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_American" class="mw-redirect" title="Icelandic American">Icelandic</a> descent, according to the 2000 US census.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Language">Language</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Iceland" title="Languages of Iceland">Languages of Iceland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_language" title="Icelandic language">Icelandic language</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_name" title="Icelandic name">Icelandic name</a></div>
<p>Iceland's official written and spoken language is <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_language" title="Icelandic language">Icelandic</a>, a <a href="/wiki/North_Germanic_languages" title="North Germanic languages">North Germanic language</a> descended from <a href="/wiki/Old_Norse" title="Old Norse">Old Norse</a>. In grammar and vocabulary, it has changed less from Old Norse than the other Nordic languages; Icelandic has preserved more verb and noun <a href="/wiki/Inflection" title="Inflection">inflection</a>, and has to a considerable extent developed new vocabulary based on native roots rather than borrowings from other languages. The puristic tendency in the development of Icelandic vocabulary is to a large degree a result of conscious language planning, in addition to centuries of isolation. Icelandic is the only living language to retain the use of the <a href="/wiki/Runic" class="mw-redirect" title="Runic">runic</a> letter <a href="/wiki/%C3%9E" class="mw-redirect" title="Þ">Þ</a> in <a href="/wiki/Latin_script" title="Latin script">Latin script</a>. The closest living relative of the Icelandic language is <a href="/wiki/Faroese_language" title="Faroese language">Faroese</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Icelandic_Sign_Language" title="Icelandic Sign Language">Icelandic Sign Language</a> was officially recognised as a minority language in 2011. In education, its use for Iceland's deaf community is <a href="/wiki/Legal_recognition_of_sign_languages" title="Legal recognition of sign languages">regulated</a> by the <i>National Curriculum Guide</i>.
</p><p>English and Danish are compulsory subjects in the school curriculum. English is widely understood and spoken, while basic to moderate knowledge of Danish is common mainly among the older generations.<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Polish_language" title="Polish language">Polish</a> is mostly spoken by the local Polish community (the largest minority of Iceland), and Danish is mostly spoken in a way largely comprehensible to Swedes and Norwegians—it is often referred to as <span title="Icelandic-language text"><i lang="is">skandinavíska</i></span> (i. e. <i>Scandinavian</i>) in Iceland.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Rather than using <a href="/wiki/Family_name" class="mw-redirect" title="Family name">family names</a>, as is the usual custom in most Western nations, Icelanders carry <a href="/wiki/Patronymic" title="Patronymic">patronymic</a> or <a href="/wiki/Matronymic" title="Matronymic">matronymic</a> surnames, patronyms being far more commonly practised. Patronymic last names are based on the first name of the father, while matronymic names are based on the first name of the mother. These follow the person's given name, e.g. <span title="Icelandic-language text"><i lang="is">Elísabet Jónsdóttir</i></span> ("Elísabet, Jón's daughter" (Jón being the father)) or <span title="Icelandic-language text"><i lang="is">Ólafur Katrínarson</i></span> ("Ólafur, Katrín's son" (Katrín being the mother)).<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> Consequently, Icelanders refer to one another by their given name, and the Icelandic telephone directory is listed alphabetically by the first name rather than by surname.<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> All new names must be approved by the <a href="/wiki/Icelandic_Naming_Committee" title="Icelandic Naming Committee">Icelandic Naming Committee</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Healthcare_in_Iceland" title="Healthcare in Iceland">Healthcare in Iceland</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Life_expectancy_in_Iceland.svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Life_expectancy_in_Iceland.svg/220px-Life_expectancy_in_Iceland.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Life_expectancy_in_Iceland.svg/330px-Life_expectancy_in_Iceland.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Life_expectancy_in_Iceland.svg/440px-Life_expectancy_in_Iceland.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="850" data-file-height="600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Life_expectancy_in_Iceland.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Life expectancy in Iceland, 1838 to 2021</div></div></div>
<p>Iceland has a <a href="/wiki/Universal_health_care" title="Universal health care">universal health care</a> system that is administered by its Ministry of Welfare (<a href="/wiki/Icelandic_language" title="Icelandic language">Icelandic</a>: <i lang="is">Velferðarráðuneytið</i>)<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> and paid for mostly by taxes (85%) and to a lesser extent by service fees (15%). Unlike most countries, there are no private hospitals, and private insurance is practically nonexistent.<sup id="cite_ref-j1_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-j1-228">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A considerable portion of the government budget is assigned to health care,<sup id="cite_ref-j1_228-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-j1-228">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> and Iceland ranks 11th in health care expenditures as a percentage of GDP<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> and 14th in spending per capita.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-230">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> Overall, the country's health care system is one of the best performing in the world, ranked 15th by the <a href="/wiki/World_Health_Organization" title="World Health Organization">World Health Organization</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> According to an OECD report, Iceland devotes far more resources to healthcare than most industrialised nations. As of 2009<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iceland&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, Iceland had 3.7 doctors per 1,000 people (compared with an average of 3.1 in OECD countries) and 15.3 nurses per 1,000 people (compared with an OECD average of 8.4).<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_230-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-230">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Icelanders are among the world's healthiest people, with 81% reporting they are in good health, according to an OECD survey.<sup id="cite_ref-Iceland_–_OECD_Better_Life_Index_112-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Iceland_–_OECD_Better_Life_Index-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Although it is a growing problem, <a href="/wiki/Obesity" title="Obesity">obesity</a> is not as prevalent as in other <a href="/wiki/Developed_countries" class="mw-redirect" title="Developed countries">developed countries</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_230-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-230">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> Iceland has many campaigns for health and wellbeing, including the famous television show <i><a href="/wiki/LazyTown" title="LazyTown">Lazytown</a></i>, starring and created by former gymnastics champion <a href="/wiki/Magn%C3%BAs_Scheving" title="Magnús Scheving">Magnus Scheving</a>. <a href="/wiki/Infant_mortality" title="Infant mortality">Infant mortality</a> is one of the lowest in the world,<sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> and the proportion of the population that smokes is lower than the OECD average.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_230-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-230">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> Almost all women choose to terminate pregnancies of children with <a href="/wiki/Down_syndrome" title="Down syndrome">Down syndrome</a> in Iceland.<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> The average <a href="/wiki/Life_expectancy" title="Life expectancy">life expectancy</a> is 81.8 (compared to an OECD average of 79.5), the fourth-highest in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Iceland has a very low level of pollution, thanks to an overwhelming reliance on cleaner geothermal energy, a low population density, and a high level of environmental consciousness among <a href="/wiki/Citizens" class="mw-redirect" title="Citizens">citizens</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> According to an OECD assessment, the amount of toxic materials in the atmosphere is far lower than in any other industrialised country measured.<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Iceland" title="Religion in Iceland">Religion in Iceland</a></div>
<table class="wikitable sortable floatright" style="font-size: 80%">
<caption style="font-size:98%">Affiliation by religious movement (1 January 2018)<sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</caption>
<tbody><tr>
<th>Affiliation
</th>
<th colspan="2">% of population
</th></tr>
<tr>
<td><b>Christianity</b>
</td>
<td align="right">78.78
</td>
